API `/api/v1/sales/shipments` (list, create, get, update, delete, post, reverse); permissions `sales.shipment.read|manage|post`, granted to `warehouse_operator` alongside its existing `purchasing.receipt.*`. Tests (`Quicker.Sales.Tests`, +6): a full shipment consuming the reservation, moving stock and booking cost of goods sold to fulfil the order (with the invariant harness after); a partial shipment across two shipments leaving the remainder reserved then completing the order; a drop-ship line and a not-yet-confirmed order both refused; a reversal returning the stock at exact cost and re-reserving it, refusing a second reversal and an empty reason; permission scoping; and the roadmap's own acceptance criterion, "Scenario 4 test on the sales path (200 parallel rounds)" — the product brief's own hard scenario 4 is a *two*-user race, so this repeats that exact race (a fresh single unit of stock, two draft shipments contending for its one reservation, posted at the same moment) 200 times over rather than staging one 200-way pile-up, which would only test the sandbox's Postgres connection budget (a hard `max_connections` ceiling, hit and reverted during this slice) rather than the engine's correctness; all 200 rounds land exactly one winner and the balance back at zero. **Web screen** (built ahead of 5.5b/5.5c at the founder's request, so there is something to see sooner): `routes/sales/ShipmentsPage.tsx`, a list-plus-dialog screen in the established pattern (nav `nav.shipments`, `s h`, `Ship` icon) — a create dialog picks a confirmed or partially-shipped order, pre-fills its shippable lines at their reserved quantity (adjustable down for a partial shipment) and posts/reverses from the detail dialog, which shows each line's booked cost of goods sold once posted; the order screen gained its own "Create shipment" button (confirmed or partially-shipped orders, `sales.shipment.manage`) that opens Shipments pre-filled through the same `?from=type:id` follow-on mechanism the drop-ship round trip already uses (`followOn("sales_order", id)`), and the order's own status/line list now shows `partially_shipped`/`shipped`/`fulfilled`. Browser journey (`e2e/sales-journeys.spec.ts`, a third scenario): a confirmed order ships in two parts through its own "Create shipment" button, the second shipment reversed and the stock and reservation given back; then Arabic, right-to-left, axe on every screen — this surfaced and fixed a real bug found only by driving the UI twice against the same order: the create dialog's own order-detail query (`["sales-order-for-shipment", orderId]`) was never invalidated after a shipment posted, so a second "Create shipment" round trip on the same order showed the pre-shipment reserved quantity instead of what was actually left; `refresh()` now invalidates that key too. Deferred to 5.5b/5.5c, listed explicitly: pick lists (FEFO/bin sequencing), a mobile picking screen, packages, carrier detail beyond the plain header fields already here, lot/serial shipment lines, and the demo seed. **5.5b part 1, lot-tracked shipments** (A-153 §11): a shipment line for a lot-tracked item now auto-picks the single earliest-expiring lot with enough available stock to cover the whole line (first-expiry-first-out), through a new cross-module contract `IFefoSuggestions` that reuses roadmap 3.5's `LotService.SuggestAsync` engine rather than duplicating it; no single lot covering the quantity is refused (`shipment.no_single_lot_covers_quantity`) rather than modelling multi-lot lines, and `sls_shipment_lines.lot_number` (migration `V0048`) stores the chosen lot by text, mirroring `pur_receipt_lines.lot_number`. Serial-tracked items are still refused outright (`shipment.serial_tracked_unsupported`, renamed from `shipment.tracked_item_unsupported`). This was the first real exercise of Sales reservation against a lot/serial-tracked item and it surfaced a genuine pre-existing bug dating to 5.4b: reservations for such an item always saw zero available (the check looked only at the item's "any lot" sentinel balance row, which never holds any of a tracked item's on-hand) and, once fixed, posting then drove the sentinel row's `reserved` negative by releasing the hold on the specific lot's own row instead — both now fixed in `ReservationService`/`StockPostingService` generally, not special-cased for Sales. Tests (`Quicker.Sales.Tests`, +2): a lot-tracked shipment picking the older, just-enough lot over the newer, larger one, and a not-a-single-lot-covers-it refusal alongside the serial-tracked refusal. Still deferred: serial-tracked shipment lines, a persisted pick-list document with bin-sequence ordering, a mobile picking screen, packages, and the demo seed (5.5c). **5.5b part 2, serial-tracked shipments and two concurrency fixes** (A-153 §12–13): a shipment line for a serial-tracked item now auto-picks that many of the item's own on-hand serials in the line's warehouse, in receipt order, through a new contract `ISerialSuggestions` mirroring `IFefoSuggestions` (ordered by the serial's own UUIDv7 id rather than its `created_at` column, since two serials from the same receipt can tie on the timestamp but never on the id); too few on hand is refused (`shipment.not_enough_serials_on_hand`); `sls_shipment_lines.serial_numbers` (migration `V0049`, jsonb) stores the chosen numbers, mirroring `pur_receipt_lines.serial_numbers`. Lot-and-serial items are still refused (`shipment.serial_tracked_unsupported`, now naming only that case). Being the first caller ever to attach a reservation to a *serial-tracked* outbound line, this surfaced a second pre-existing bug: `TrackingResolver`'s serial branch built each unit's resolved line with its reservation stripped, so a serial-tracked shipment's reservation was silently never consumed, permanently stuck holding its quantity forever; fixed by resolving the reservation once for the whole line and sharing it (and the row its hold lives on) across every per-unit resolved line. Rerunning 5.5a's own 200-round concurrency scenario after both fixes found a third, unrelated pre-existing race: the reservation's remaining-quantity check read it unlocked, so the losing side of two shipments racing the same reservation could over-consume it and hit the database's own `reserved` check constraint as an unhandled 500 instead of the ordinary `stock.reservation_exceeded` 409 (data was always correct either way — the failed transaction rolled back cleanly — but the error surfaced ugly); fixed by locking the reservation row before that check, plus an explicit reload since EF's identity map would otherwise hand back a stale already-tracked copy of the same row. Tests (`Quicker.Sales.Tests`, +1, replacing the old "serial refused outright" case with a lot-and-serial one): a serial-tracked shipment picking the oldest on-hand serials, refusing a quantity with too few in stock, and a reversal returning the exact serials to stock. Still deferred: a lot-and-serial item, a persisted pick-list document with bin-sequence ordering, a mobile picking screen, packages, and the demo seed (5.5c). **5.5b part 3, pick lists, handheld picking, packages and lot-and-serial lines** (A-154): the inventory module gains pick lists (`inv_pick_lists`/`inv_pick_lines`, migration `V0050`) behind a new cross-module contract `IPickLists` (plan, release, find, cancel, close). Its planner locates a line lot by lot (first expiry first out for an item with the FEFO flag, first received first out otherwise — the flag existed since 3.1 but nothing read it), then bin by bin in the bins' pick sequence (the walking order; only storage and shipping bins), serials oldest received first, never counting stock another live pick list claims or another document reserved on that bin, lot or serial; releases take a per-item advisory lock so two never plan the same shelf. The handheld API (`/inventory/pick-lists`: queue, claim, assign, pick a line with the bin/lot/serials actually taken, short-pick with a reason, reset, cancel; permissions `inventory.pick.read`, `.execute`, `.manage`) records what was picked; nothing moves until the shipment posts. Shipments (migration `V0051`) keep per-line `allocations` (bin, lot, serials, quantity and, once posted, each part's ledger entries — backfilled for shipments posted earlier); a draft can post directly (located at that moment) or be `release`d to the warehouse (`picking`, edits refused until `cancel-picking`), and posting ships exactly what was picked: a short pick reduces the line and the rest stays reserved on the order. A line now splits across lots and bins, and a lot-and-serial item ships (lot first, then serials within it); the old refusals `shipment.no_single_lot_covers_quantity` and `shipment.serial_tracked_unsupported` are gone. Reversal returns every ledger entry one by one at its own cost, back to its bin and lot (replacing A-153 §12's first-entry simplification). Packages (`sls_shipment_packages` + `sls_shipment_package_lines`): type, weight, dimensions and tracking number per package, contents by order line never beyond what the shipment carries (checked on packing, on editing and again after short picks), editable after posting like the new `PUT .../carrier`. A shipment leaves from one warehouse (`shipment.one_warehouse`). Found and fixed on the way (see post-milestone fixes): a sales order could not be confirmed for a warehouse with bins at all. Tests (`Quicker.Sales.Tests`, 6 new in `PickingTests`, the lot test now states its FEFO policy, the obsolete refusal test replaced; full solution run 394 passed, 1 skipped (the S3 storage test that needs an object store), 0 failed): walking order across bins with a receiving bin skipped, a picker-only member working the list, a short pick shipping 9 of 10 with the tenth still reserved, reversal back to each bin; two releases planning different shelves, an off-plan pick refused where another list holds the stock, claim/assign rules, cancel-picking and re-release, delete cancelling the list; two releases fired at once for the same shelf, 25 rounds, always planning different bins; packages over-packed, of an unknown type, shrunk under by an edit, packed and re-carried after posting, refused once reversed; a lot-and-serial item taking the earlier-expiring lot's serials first and returning each serial on reversal; a line split across lots FEFO and FIFO. Still deferred to 5.5c: the web screens (picking and packing panels on the shipment, the handheld picking screen), the browser journey and the demo seed. **5.5c, screens, journeys and demo** (A-154): the shipment screen shows where each line comes from (bin, lot, expiry, serials), releases a draft for picking or takes it back, shows its pick list's progress and posts once every line is picked; packages are edited on the shipment (type, weight, size, tracking, contents by line, with a packed-vs-shipped check) and the carrier details stay editable after posting. A new Inventory › Pick lists screen is the warehouse's work queue (who has each list, lines done, picked vs planned), with assign, take, cancel and open-on-scanner. The scanner gains a Pick tab: the picker takes a list and walks it in bin order, scanning bin, item and each serial, confirming or short-picking with a reason, with the route and undo below (online only: it checks live stock). Pick lists now carry the picker's name and each line's unit. EN and AR throughout. Browser journeys: `mobile-picking.spec.ts` (phone: walk the list, wrong item refused, a short pick, the office posts 7 of 8) and `picking-packing.spec.ts` (desktop: allocations, release, pick list screen, post, two cartons, carrier; Arabic), axe on every screen. Demo seed (`DemoShipping`): four orders from the Baghdad main warehouse (bins): one picked with one line short, posted, packed in two cartons with tracking; one half picked by the warehouse operator; one released; one draft (groceries lines lot-tracked, FEFO); the groceries category is classified standard-rated for tax. Found on the way: three tax strings used `{{name}}`, which the ICU format shows literally, and CI never ran the Arabic-completeness check that would have caught it; both fixed (the check now runs in the web CI job). |
| 5.4 Quotations, orders, credit control and drop-ship | done | **5.4a, quotations** (A-151): new module `Quicker.Sales` (tier 2, `Quicker.Sales`/`.Contracts`/`.Tests`/`.TestSupport`), migration `V0044__sales_quotations.sql` (`sls_quotations`, `sls_quotation_lines`). A quotation is keyed by `partner_id` (not the `customer_account_id` DOMAIN_MODEL §11 originally sketched — `ICustomerDirectory` has no separate account id, so it follows Purchasing's `pur_orders.partner_id` exactly); `EnsureCustomerAsync(..., CustomerPurposes.Quote)` gates it (an account on hold still quotes — holding an order is 5.4b's decision). Every save prices lines through `IPricing.PriceAsync` and taxes them through `ITaxDetermination.CalculateAsync`, refusing an unpriced line; each line freezes its tax rate, reverse-charge, recoverable and reason at save time (A-147's precedent) rather than re-deriving them at read time, so a later tax change never rewrites a sent or accepted quotation. Draft → sent → accepted/rejected/expired; only a draft is editable; accepting past `ValidUntil` (the company's own time zone) is refused (`quotation.expired`); rejecting needs a reason. API `/api/v1/sales/quotations` (list, create, get, update, send, accept, reject). Permissions `sales.quote.read|manage`; also forward-declares `sales.order.manage` and `sales.invoice.post` (no endpoint checks them yet) because two default SoD rules from M4 already pair them with `partners.credit.manage` and `receivables.writeoff.post` — Identity's own test on the default rules requires that once any `sales.*` permission ships, every `sales.*` key a rule names must exist in the catalogue, so a rule from an already-shipped module can never go silently dead. Tests (`Quicker.Sales.Tests`, 4): full create→send→accept flow pricing two lines from the items' own list price with the invariant harness after, reject with a required reason, accept refused past validity, permission scoping (read-only lists and gets but cannot create). **5.4b, orders, reservations and credit control** (A-152, hard scenario 6): migration `V0045__sales_orders.sql` (`sls_orders`, `sls_order_lines`), superseding DOMAIN_MODEL §11's Phase-0 `sls_credit_holds` table — the credit check reuses the generic workflow block/override mechanism `pur_invoices` already use for match variance (ADR-0020), with `block_kind`/`block_id`/`block_reason`/`override_id` columns on the order instead of a bespoke one; `app.wf_overrides` already logs who released a hold, when and why. An order is created directly (priced and taxed exactly like a quotation) or converted from an accepted quotation, whose frozen lines carry over verbatim (the quotation moves to `converted` with its `order_id` set) rather than silently re-pricing a commitment already made. Confirming an order reserves each line's remaining quantity through `IStockReservations.ReserveAsync`; a line short of stock reserves only what `AvailabilityAsync` shows and stays `backordered` (`POST .../retry-backorders` re-attempts it) rather than blocking the whole order; fulfilment quantities are tracked in the item's base unit (not the line's own, unlike Purchasing's `qty_received`) so a partial reservation never has to run `ItemUomMath`'s exact-conversion check the wrong way; partial and whole-order cancellation release reservations and, once confirmed, re-reserve what remains. The customer's credit exposure (the sum of their other confirmed orders in the same currency, plus this one — `open_ar_plus_orders` with the `open_ar` half honestly at zero until Receivables exists) is checked only when a limit is set; over it, `ConsumeOverrideAsync`/`RaiseBlockAsync` hold the order (`on_hold`, `credit_limit`) for an authorized approver exactly as an invoice's price variance does. Confirming two orders of the same customer at once can never both pass an exposure only one should: the confirm transaction takes `pg_advisory_xact_lock` on (tenant, company, partner) before reading the exposure sum (the same locking idiom migrations V0003/V0011 already use for partitions), serializing concurrent confirmations for one customer. API adds `/api/v1/sales/orders` (list, create, get, confirm, retry-backorders, cancel, line-cancel) and `POST /api/v1/sales/quotations/{id}/convert`; permissions `sales.quote.read|manage` promoted from forward-declared to enforced, plus a new `sales.order.read`. Tests (`Quicker.Sales.Tests`, +6, 10 total): convert-and-confirm reserving stock with the invariant harness after, a short line backordering then fulfilling on retry, partial then whole cancellation releasing reservations, the full credit-hold-to-override-to-confirm flow through a real workflow definition and approval, two orders of the same customer confirmed with `Task.WhenAll` always landing exactly one `confirmed` and one `on_hold` (stable across repeated runs), permission scoping. **5.4c, drop-ship** (A-152 (6), migration `V0046__sales_drop_ship.sql`): a line flagged `drop_ship` skips reservation on confirmation entirely (straight to `open`; the supplier ships to the customer, never the company's own warehouse) and takes a purchase order line's id once it is raised (`POST /api/v1/sales/orders/{id}/lines/{lineId}/purchase-order`, refused a second time with `order.line_already_linked`) — creating that purchase order is a web-layer next-step button, the same `?from=type:id` pattern Purchasing's own document flow already uses, not a new backend call between the two modules. Tests (`Quicker.Sales.Tests`, +1, 11 total): a drop-ship line with no stock at all still confirms straight to `open`, links a purchase order line once, refuses a second link. **5.4c, web screens and the drop-ship round trip**: Quotations and Orders under a new "SALES AND CRM" nav pair (`nav.quotations` `s q`, `nav.salesOrders` `s o`), each a list-plus-dialog screen in the established pattern (`QuotationsPage.tsx`, `OrdersPage.tsx`, shared line editor and read-only lines table in `sales/shared.tsx`), EN/AR throughout. A quotation's dialog carries send/accept/reject and, once accepted, "convert to order" (asks only for the fulfilling warehouse, per `ConvertQuotationRequest`); an order's dialog carries confirm/retry-backorders/cancel-line/cancel-order, a credit-hold banner (the block's own reason plus a link to the approvals inbox — granting the override itself is the generic workflow screens' job, already proven by `workflow-journeys.spec.ts`) and, per drop-ship line, a "create purchase order" button. That button is the "web-layer next step" A-152 promised, now real: it opens Purchasing's own new-order form pre-filled (item, quantity, unit, warehouse, a note naming the sales order) through the same `?from=type:id` follow-on mechanism Purchasing's receipts/invoices already use for their own document flow (`sales_order_line:<orderId>:<lineId>`, parsed back apart in `PurchaseOrdersPage.tsx`); saving that purchase order calls the sales order's own link endpoint automatically with the new line's id and returns the browser to the sales order, which now shows the line linked — two independent REST calls made from the browser, never a new cross-module call on the server. Browser journey (`e2e/sales-journeys.spec.ts`, a second scenario alongside 5.1c's): a quotation sent, accepted, converted; the order confirmed (full reservation), a line part-cancelled and re-reserved; a second order backordered against the same stock, then retried once more arrives; a third order held for the customer's credit limit and cancelled instead of overridden; a fourth drop-ships a never-stocked item, is confirmed with nothing reserved, and its purchase order is raised and linked from the order screen itself; then the same screens in Arabic, right-to-left, every step passing axe. **Demo** (`DemoSales`): four quotations for Iraq Trading's customers (two of Baghdad Mall's still waiting on a decision, Kurdistan Distribution's accepted, converted and confirmed, Al-Noor's rejected on price) and a direct order for Basra Oil mixing a normally stocked line with an unlinked drop-ship one; every line priced and taxed by the pricing and tax engines already seeded, needing the BEV/ACC/STAT categories classified with the "STANDARD" item tax group first (an unclassified item is refused by tax determination, `tax.item_unclassified`) — exactly what an admin going live with tax would do. Demo seeder test asserts four quotations, two confirmed orders and one still-unlinked drop-ship line, deterministic on reseed. |

| Item | Status | Notes |
|------|--------|-------|
| Cost explanation screen | done | Double-clicking a movement on the stock ledger opens "Cost of this movement" over `GET /inventory/costing/entries/{sleId}`: quantity, unit cost, cost and whether it stands at expected or actual cost; its value entries (kind, quantities, actual and expected amounts, account and offset roles, source); the layers it took cost from or gave cost to (current, re-applied, superseded); and the cost runs that touched it. The ledger's source column reads as words. English and Arabic; the inventory journey opens it and runs axe on it. |

English and Arabic; the admin journeys create a series (a template without `{seq}` refused first) and open the screen right to left, with axe. |

The API client now answers `auth.step_up_required` with a "Confirm it's you" dialog (password, or an authenticator code for members with MFA), calls `POST /me/step-up`, keeps the session's refresh token, and replays the same request with the fresh token, so the action completes in one click; cancelling returns the original refusal. Concurrent requests share one prompt. A unit test covers the replay and the cancel; the settings journey shortens the window to a minute, waits it out and creates a key through the prompt against the real API. |

New roles start empty or from a template (the grants that exist today are copied, the reserved ones named). | E-invoicing: ZATCA phase 2 (founder programme item 6; roadmap 8.6) | done | New process module `Quicker.Einvoicing` (A-179, ADR-0018 amended, migration `V0065__einvoicing.sql`): `einv_devices` and `einv_submissions`; the ZATCA adapter registered behind the tax module's `ITaxClearanceProvider`, fed the full document by `IEinvoiceSource` (Sales implements it for invoices and credit notes: seller from the company and its VAT registration, buyer from the partner's VAT registration and billing address, lines with the tax category from the code's treatment, the credited invoice of a credit note); UBL 2.1 in the ZATCA profile (type code with the standard or simplified flags, ICV and PIH references, billing reference and instruction note of a credit or debit note, the two tax totals, monetary totals, lines with their tax category); a secp256k1 key and certificate request per device with the scheme's alternative name; the invoice hash on the canonical XML without extensions, signature and QR (own canonicalizer held to vectors), the previous-hash chain under a row lock on the device; the XAdES signature block and TLV QR of simplified invoices, the QR of a cleared standard invoice taken from the scheme's answer; the API client for compliance and production certificates, compliance checks, clearance and reporting against the sandbox, simulation and production addresses; an in-process simulator (certificates from a built-in authority, the validator's checks on hash, UUID, counter, seller VAT, totals, the signature against the certificate and the QR against hash and signature) selected by a device's environment; device onboarding (request, compliance certificate with OTP, six compliance samples, production certificate, renewal, revocation) with secrets protected; posting an invoice under a scheme enqueues its submission (`sales.invoices.einvoice`), a manual device leaves it pending; permissions `einvoicing.device.read/manage`, `einvoicing.submission.read` (accountant `einvoicing.*`, sales manager and receivables clerk read submissions); screen `/tax/einvoicing` (devices with their status bar and actions, submissions with warnings, errors, the QR and the signed and cleared XML) and the QR with a link on the invoice's e-invoice tab, English and Arabic; `ZatcaCryptoTests` (canonical form, first hash, request, hash unchanged by signing, signature verified, QR read back, credit note layout), `ZatcaDeviceTests` (onboarding to production against the simulator, a standard invoice cleared with the scheme's QR, a simplified one reported with its own, a credit note, the chain, renewal and revocation; a standard-only device refusing a cash sale and a manual device refusing automatic submission) and journey `einvoicing-journeys.spec.ts`. |
| Transfer quantities | done | A draft transfer can be shipped in part ("Ship part…": per line, up to the requested quantity; what is not shipped is not sent later) and a shipped one received with differences ("Receive with differences…": per line, what arrived, what is short with a shortage reason and the note the reason may require, and the destination bin when it has bins; anything left stays in transit for a later receipt). Serial-tracked lines are settled serial by serial. Limits are checked as they are typed with exact decimal arithmetic (`lib/decimal.ts`, BigInt, never floating point; unit-tested), and the server checks again. "Ship" and "Receive" still take everything in one click. English and Arabic; a new transfer journey ships 35 of 40, receives 20 with 5 lost in transit (reason and note), then the remaining 10, and checks the lines, status and stock by warehouse, with axe. It surfaced open issue I3 (same-day receipts under average cost). |
| Chart CSV and statutory mapping | done | The chart screen exports the chart as CSV (one row per account, the columns the import reads; the endpoint now declares `text/csv`) and imports a CSV file or pasted rows: accounts upserted by code, parents after children allowed, and the whole file refused with the reason when one row is wrong; the result counts created, updated and unchanged rows. "Statutory mapping" maps each postable account to an account of a statutory chart (the Iraqi unified chart is seeded), shows how many are not mapped, filters to those, and saves only the rows changed (an emptied row removes its mapping). English and Arabic; the accounting tools journey exports, re-imports with a new account after a refused file, and maps it, with axe. |
| Journal import and attachments | done | "Import CSV" on the journals screen reads journals from a file or pasted rows (one row per line, grouped by `journal_ref`; the required and optional columns are listed) and creates each as a draft to review, submit and post; a wrong row refuses the whole file with the reason. Every journal shows its attachments: attach a file, download it, remove it (a reusable panel over the collaboration attachments API, whose upload endpoint now declares its multipart form in OpenAPI). English and Arabic; the accounting tools journey imports a two-line journal and attaches, downloads and removes a supporting document, with axe. |
| Item structure | done | The item dialog gains a picture (uploaded as an attachment of the item and set as its image, shown in the header, removable) and three tabs. **Bill of material** (kit and assembly items): the versions with what each makes and whether it is active, the components of the selected one with unit and scrap, a new version started from the selected one (saved active or not; activating one retires the other), and the requirements exploded for any quantity through sub-assemblies, in base units with and without scrap. **Variants**: a SKU per combination of attribute values with its name and status; the server refuses a combination already used. **Substitutes**: items that may replace this one, in order, reordered and removed. Variant attributes and their values are defined next to categories and brands ("Categories, brands and attributes"). English and Arabic; the item structure journey builds a six-pack's two BOM versions and explodes ten (60 bottles, 63 caps with 5% scrap), adds a size attribute and two variants after a refused duplicate, a substitute and a picture, with axe (A-121). |
| Comments, history and the audit event | done | A record's **Discussion** tab holds its comments and, where the member may see them, its files: threads with replies, mentions picked from the workspace's members (the pick writes "@Name" into the text, shows who will be notified as removable chips, and notifies them), edits marked as such and removal after a confirmation, by the author or a member with `collaboration.comment.manage`; a removed comment keeps its place so replies still read in context; Ctrl+Enter sends. Whom a comment can mention comes from a new `GET /api/v1/collaboration/comments/mentionable` (active members by name only, for anyone with `collaboration.comment.write`; the member list itself needs `identity.user.read`). The **History** tab merges the record's audit trail (each event with its action, who, when, reason and the fields it changed from and to; long change lists fold) with its activity (comments, files, links), newest first; each half needs its own permission. Any audit event opens in full (request, correlation, address and client, the change table, the whole record before and after, details, and its place in the hash chain), from the history or the **audit explorer**, whose actions and record types now read as words in English and Arabic (suggested in the filters, typed codes still work) and which narrows to every event of one record from an event. `GET /audit/events/{id}` now declares its response in the contract. The tabs are on purchase orders, supplier invoices, requests for quotation, landed costs, suppliers, items and journals; the other documents (requisitions, receipts, returns, agreements, payments, payment proposals and the five stock documents) open the same discussion and history from two toggles under their details, loaded only when asked for (the transfer journey comments on a transfer and reads its history). English and Arabic; the record discussion journey comments on an item with a mention, replies, edits and removes, renames the item and reads the change in its history and in the event, finds the mention in the inbox, filters the explorer by action and narrows it to the item, then reads it right to left, with axe (A-123). |
| Find a document by number | done | **Command palette** (Ctrl/⌘+K) gains a Documents group: any issued number, in full ("PO-2026-00027"), in part ("00027") or as a prefix with an unpadded sequence ("PO-27", "GRN-1"), across every numbered type (requisitions, RFQs, agreements, orders, receipts, supplier invoices, landed costs, returns, stock adjustments, revaluations, assemblies, transfers, counts, manual journals, journal entries, supplier payments, payment proposals), each hit with its type, company and date; Enter opens the document on its screen. Backed by `GET /api/v1/numbering/documents?q=&limit=` over the numbering allocation log (trigram index, `V0034`), the exact number first, then the sequence match, then prefix, then newest; each module registers its numbered types with their read permission (`NumberedDocumentTypes`), and a member sees only the types they may read, in the companies their grant covers (A-124). **Every document opens from a link**: the purchasing, banking and payables screens now keep the open record in the URL (`?open=id`) as the inventory and accounting screens already did, so the palette, a ledger line, an audit event or the back button reach it. **Source documents from the books**: a ledger line and a journal entry name their source document by number and open it (receipts, supplier invoices, landed costs, returns, stock documents, payments and manual journals; before, only manual journals linked); entries posted by the costing engine do not carry their source's number, so the entry, the browser (whose number filter now finds them too) and the ledger read it from numbering through a new read-only contract (`IIssuedNumbers`). **Audit events** opened from the explorer offer "Open the record". English and Arabic; the document search journey finds a posted receipt by "GRN-1" and its order by full number, opens the receipt from its journal entry's source and from its audit event, then reads the palette right to left, with axe; API tests cover ranking, partial and sequence matches, validation, the limit, permission and company filtering and tenant isolation, and the receipt test checks the source number in entry, browser and ledger. |
| Journal line details | done | Each line of the manual journal editor opens its details: a value for every dimension of the company (cost centre, department, project and custom ones; the branch stays the journal's), with the account's rules applied (required marked, blocked left out, the default named in the empty choice), the supplier or customer when the account is the payables or receivables control account (a reference field for other control accounts), and the line's own description; a count on the toggle shows how many are set. The journal's detail shows them under each line (dimension values by code and name, the partner, the description). **Fixed on the way:** editing a journal kept only each line's account and amounts, so saving an edit silently dropped its dimensions, subledger item and line descriptions; the editor now carries them all, and the totals add as exact decimals rather than floating point. English and Arabic; the journal lines journey drafts rent against a supplier's payable, is refused at posting for the missing cost centre, adds the cost centre and supplier, keeps them through a second edit, posts, and reads the lines right to left, with axe (A-125). |
| Security and access screens | done | **Segregation-of-duties exceptions** are listed on the Security screen (member, conflicting pair, reason, who approved it and when, end date, status in force, expired or revoked) and an exception in force can be **revoked** with a reason: the row stays with who revoked it, when and why, and the conflict counts again at once (`GET /api/v1/sod/exceptions`, `POST /api/v1/sod/exceptions/{id}/revoke`, `V0035`). **Roles from SSO groups**: a connection names its group claim and maps groups to roles in its dialog. **Allowed networks**: the sign-in policy edits the workspace IP allow-list (addresses and CIDR ranges, IPv4 and IPv6, at most 100), which was stored but never applied; it is now enforced on sign-in (after the password, `auth.ip_not_allowed`), on MFA, on refresh, on SSO and on every request of a member's session (API keys keep their own list). A list that leaves out the address the change comes from is refused (`tenant.policy_ip_lockout`, naming the address), and unreadable entries are named (`tenant.policy_ip_invalid`). The client address is the connection's; a forwarded one is believed only from proxies named in `Quicker:Api:TrustedProxies` (before, sign-in took `X-Forwarded-For` from any caller). **A role limited to several places**: the member dialog ticks any number of companies, branches and warehouses for one assignment. English and Arabic; API tests cover the allow-list end to end (lockout, invalid entries, normalisation, ranges, single addresses, IPv6, unknown addresses, sign-in and refresh from outside, a trusted proxy and a spoofed header, clearing) and revocation; the access journey limits a role to two companies and revokes the exception, the settings journey maps a group and saves an allow-list after being told its own address (A-126). |
| Outbound request guard | done | Webhook deliveries and single sign-on calls (discovery, keys, the code exchange) reach **public internet addresses only**. `PublicNetworkPolicy` (`Quicker.Web`) resolves the destination itself and connects only to allowed addresses, so a name that later resolves inward (DNS rebinding) or a redirect cannot get round it; refused: loopback, private ranges, link-local and cloud metadata (169.254.169.254), carrier-grade NAT, multicast, documentation and reserved ranges, IPv6 unique-local, link-local and site-local, and IPv6 forms that embed IPv4 (mapped, compatible, NAT64, 6to4, Teredo). Operators allow named internal networks with `Quicker:Outbound:AllowedPrivateNetworks`. Saving a webhook whose address is, or resolves only to, a refused one fails with `webhook.url_blocked` (shown on the URL field); a delivery refused at connection time is marked failed at once, without a day of retries. A single sign-on start or callback against an unreachable or refused provider answers `sso.provider_unreachable` instead of a server error. Webhook clients no longer follow redirects. Tests cover the address table, operator exceptions, a refused and an allowed real connection, save-time refusals and a delivery refused after the address changed, and a provider on a private network (A-127). |
| Outbox dead letters: discard | done | A dead-lettered integration event can be **discarded** by a platform operator with a reason (`POST /api/v1/platform/ops/outbox/{id}/discard`): its handlers never run, the message keeps who discarded it, when and why (`V0036`), and the later events of its aggregate, which waited behind it, are delivered. A discarded message cannot be retried (its successors may already be out) and is archived with the published ones after the retention. The outbox list gains a `discarded` state and the dead list leaves discarded ones out. **Operator screen**: the Jobs screen shows platform operators the undelivered events (dead letters, discarded, waiting) with Retry and Discard. **Operators** are granted by the migrator: `quicker-migrate operator --email ADDRESS [--revoke]`. English and Arabic; the outbox test dead-letters an event, is refused without operator rights and without a reason, discards it, sees the next event of the aggregate delivered and the old handler never run, and archives it; a web test drives the screen (A-128). |
| Routine run log | done | Every run of the daily accounting routines, from the daily schedule or "Run routines now", is **logged per company** in the same transaction as what it posted (`app.gl_routine_runs`, append-only, `V0037`): when, for which date, the schedule or the member who ran it, how many items went out and how many waited, and each item (the entry automatically reversed, the recurring template, the deferral schedule) with what it produced or the problem code it waited on. A tenant-wide run logs a row for every company, so a day with nothing due still shows the routines ran. `GET /api/v1/accounting/companies/{id}/routine-runs` (journal read). **Run history** tab on Recurring & deferrals: runs newest first, each opening to its items with links to the journal or entry produced. English and Arabic; the journal tests check the log of three runs (who, date, the reversal with the reversed and produced numbers) and of a run generating two recurring journals; the accounting journey runs the routines and reads the history. |
| Grid export | done | Every list exports **what it shows** to Excel (.xlsx) or CSV: its visible columns in their order, the selected rows (or all) in the current sort, localized texts in the reader's language, numbers as number cells and dates as date cells, right to left in Arabic. The server writes the file (`POST /api/v1/exports/table`, the same writer as the trial balance and ledger exports, up to 50,000 rows), refuses a role whose document-type rules deny `export` for the listed kind (`export.not_allowed`), and records each export in the audit trail (format, rows, columns). CSV text a spreadsheet would run as a formula (`=`, `+`, `-`, `@` first) is written inert with a leading apostrophe, for these exports and the report exports alike. Downloads keep their server-given names, Arabic ones included (`Content-Disposition` is now exposed to the web origin). English and Arabic; API tests cover CSV (byte order mark, quoting, inert formulas, Arabic), XLSX (typed number and date cells, right to left, text never a formula), validation, the export rule and the audit; the admin journey downloads the companies list as CSV and, in Arabic, as a workbook (A-129). |
| Typed contract | done | The last 24 operations without a described success response are typed: DTOs for the TOTP enrolment and its confirmation, WebAuthn registration options, the audit chain status and anchors, period resolution, draft identifiers, job types, the SoD exception created and document links; binary bodies for file downloads (attachments, audit and list exports); and the real status for the bodiless ones (202 for the password reset request, 302 for the single sign-on start, 204 for sign-out, job retry and cancel, schedule removal and outbox retry and discard), each recorded as a correction since the document had said 200. `check-api-contract` now fails on any operation whose success response is undescribed, from the first contract on. |
| Posted accounts keep their meaning | done | Once an account has journal lines, an edit or a chart import that would change what those lines mean is refused with the field it concerns: its type (`account.type_locked`, which statement the balance belongs to), becoming a header (`account.is_header_locked`), its control flag or subledger (`account.subledger_type_locked`), a currency restriction its lines are not in (`account.currency_restriction_locked`, naming the currencies) or a company restriction other companies' lines break (`account.company_id_locked`); the import names the row. Renaming, regrouping under a header of the same type, category, cash-flow class, default role, future manual posting and activation stay editable, and a restriction the lines already satisfy is accepted. A malformed JSON import body now answers `import.body_required` rather than a server error. The account editor shows the refusal on the field. Tests: the same account changes freely before its first posting and is held after it, by edit and by import (A-130). |
| Purchasing document flow | done | Every purchasing document shows **smart buttons** for the documents linked to it along the chain, with a count, as in the major ERPs: a purchase order its requisitions, RFQ, blanket agreement, goods receipts, returns, landed costs, supplier invoices and debit notes; a receipt, invoice, return or landed cost the orders it belongs to and everything else on them. One linked document opens directly; several open a list with number, status, date and amount. `GET /api/v1/purchasing/document-flow/{documentType}/{documentId}` (`DocumentFlowService`) finds the orders a document belongs to and the documents around them, counting only documents of a type the user may read and of a company their scopes allow (A-131); the document itself answers 404 when it is not readable, an unknown type 422 `document_flow.type_invalid`. **Next steps in one click**: an approved, sent or partly received order offers *Receive* (a new receipt with the order and its open lines filled in, into the order's or the only warehouse) and *Create invoice*; a posted receipt offers *Create invoice* (its uninvoiced lines) and *Return goods* (a return of that receipt); a posted return with uncredited lines offers *Create debit note* (a debit note with its lines). Each opens the next screen with `?from=type:id`, fills the new document from its source (in the source's company, supplier and currency) and says so when nothing is left to receive, invoice or return; the buttons show only with the permission to create the next document. Open invoicable lines now carry the receipt, return and landed-cost document they come from (`receiptId`, `returnId`, `landedCostId`, additive). EN/AR. Tests: `DocumentFlowTests` (2: a requisition → order → receipt → return → invoice → debit note chain seen the same from every document, an expense invoice standing alone, the RFQ award chain, 422/404, an order-only reader who sees only orders and gets 404 on the RFQ); the purchasing journey now receives from the order's button, invoices from the receipt's, credits the return from its own and checks the order's smart buttons and the receipt they open. |
| My account and two-step sign-in | done | **My account** (`/account`, from the account menu, for every signed-in person) over the existing `/me` services: the profile (display name, language, number style and time zone; saving switches the app to them), the password (current, new and repeated; the other sessions are signed out), **two-step verification** (status, the methods with when each was added and last used, add an **authenticator app** with a QR code and the key typed out, add a **security key or passkey** by name through the browser's WebAuthn prompt, remove a method, new recovery codes; recovery codes are shown once with copy and download) and **where you are signed in** (device, address, how the session was signed in, when, last active; sign out one or all other sessions). **Sign-in**: the two-step step now offers the security key when the person has one, takes a recovery code in the same field as the authenticator code (six digits are a code, anything else a recovery code; before this a recovery code typed there was refused), and a workspace that requires two-step verification has a person without it set up an authenticator app while signing in (QR code, confirm, recovery codes, then the next code) instead of stopping at "unexpected answer". The QR code is drawn from `uqr` (MIT, no dependencies) as SVG (A-132). EN/AR. Tests: `lib/webauthn` (4: base64url, registration and sign-in options, failure kinds), the login step (recovery code and code sent as such, the key offered); **the account journeys run a real WebAuthn ceremony in Chromium's virtual authenticator** against the server's verification (registration, then signing in with the key), sign in with a recovery code once and not twice, with the next authenticator code, change the password and switch the page to Arabic, and a workspace requiring two-step verification enrols its owner while signing in. The journeys run on `localhost` (browsers refuse security keys on an IP address), which CI allows as an origin next to the address. |
| Step-up with a security key | done | Once someone has two-step verification, confirming a sensitive action (step-up) no longer accepts the password alone, and it accepted only an authenticator code, so **a person whose only second factor was a security key could not confirm anything** (new recovery codes, removing a method, API keys, SSO, the sign-in policy…). Step-up now also takes a key: `POST /api/v1/me/step-up/webauthn/options` issues the challenge and `POST /me/step-up` takes `webAuthnOptionsId` and `webAuthnResponse` next to `password` and `code` (additive). The confirm-identity dialog offers the key when the person has one and, when a key is all they have, only the key. Tests: `WebAuthnTests` (2, against the real verification with a software P-256 authenticator: a key registered, its registration options refused a second time, sign-in with it recorded as `pwd mfa hwk` and its last use, step-up after the window refused with the password and accepted with the key; a registration answered for another site and a forged signature refused, the sign-in still completing with a real one); the step-up dialog test; an account journey where a key-only person waits out a one-minute window and confirms new recovery codes with the key in Chromium's virtual authenticator. |
| Record permissions for comments and files | done | Comments, files, a record's timeline and its links were readable by anyone holding the collaboration permissions, on any record: a member without access to supplier invoices could read an invoice's discussion and download its attached PDF by id. Each module now registers the read permission of its record types (`RecordReadPermission` in `Collaboration.Contracts`: the eight purchasing documents, stock adjustments, transfers, counts, assemblies and revaluations, warehouses, items, suppliers, companies, manual journals, supplier payments and bank accounts, payment proposals), and the collaboration services check it: lists and new entries on a record type the member may not read are refused with `403 record.read_forbidden` (naming the type and the permissions, no record), a comment, file or link opened by id answers 404, and a record's links leave out those that lead to records the member may not read. Links made by the modules themselves are not limited by who is working. Tests: `RecordAccessTests` (a clerk with every collaboration permission but not `purchasing.order.read` refused on the order's comments, files, timeline and links, the file and the comment by id not found, the link to the order hidden from an open record; a buyer with the permission sees and adds to all of it). |
| Demo: the rest of the purchasing chain | done | The demo seed (`DemoPurchasing`) adds, per company and through the same services: a shelf-restock requisition from the warehouse, ordered from its suggested supplier and delivered in two parts, each invoiced (so the order's smart buttons list two receipts and two invoices, and the requisition leads to all of them); a half-year blanket agreement at a fixed price, activated, with a first release of 100 units still to arrive; an order whose invoice came in five percent above the order price and is **blocked** by the match, with an override request waiting in Approvals for the demo's Approver (Layla Ahmed, `approvals@quicker.example`) because a workflow rule routes price-variance blocks to the Approver role with a comment; and an approved requisition waiting for a buyer to press "Create orders". The demo test counts them (orders 3 × 39, invoices 3 × 7, the extra receipt lines, requisition statuses, the agreement's release, the three blocked invoices and their three pending override requests) and the harness stays green on seed and reseed. |
| Replenishment to purchase orders | done | Selected replenishment suggestions (or one from its detail) become **draft purchase orders** in one step: `POST /api/v1/purchasing/orders/from-suggestions` (`ReplenishmentOrderService`, permission `purchasing.order.manage`) makes one order per company, supplier and warehouse, each line in the item's base unit for the quantity decided (or suggested), wanted by the suggestion's date, at the price the supplier last charged for the item on an order in the supplier's currency (zero when never, for the buyer to price before submitting); suggestions without a preferred supplier take the supplier the buyer chooses (`replenishment.supplier_missing` otherwise, naming the item). Each suggestion is accepted with that quantity and supplier and records its order line through the new `IReplenishmentSuggestions` contract of the inventory module; one already ordered, dismissed or superseded is refused (`replenishment.not_orderable`). Once the order is approved the planner counts it as incoming and stops suggesting the item. The replenishment screen gains selection, a Supplier column, "Create purchase orders" (with the supplier choice when needed) and a result listing the new orders as links; decided suggestions show "On order". EN/AR. Tests: a purchasing test (tea from its preferred supplier at the last price 12.50, coffee refused without a supplier then ordered from the chosen one at zero, both suggestions carrying their lines and refused a second time, the approved order stopping the next suggestion for tea, harness green); the replenishment ordering journey. |
| Open order lines | done | **Purchasing → Open order lines** (`/purchasing/open-lines`, `g *`, permission `purchasing.order.read`, over `GET /api/v1/purchasing/reports/open-order-lines`): the stock lines of approved, sent and partly received orders with goods still to arrive (ordered less cancelled less received), with supplier, item, warehouse, expected date (the line's, else the order's), ordered, received and open quantities, the net unit price and the open value in the order's currency; lines past their expected date show how many days late they are. Filters: company (with the member's company scope), supplier, warehouse, item, as-of date, late only; totals per currency and the count of late lines; a line opens its order; the grid exports. Service lines, which are invoiced rather than received, are left out. EN/AR. Tests: a purchasing test (ten tea due on the 5th with six received and four coffee at 10 % off due on the 20th: 4 × 100 = 400 five days late and 4 × 45 = 180 on time, totals 580, the late filter, supplier and item filters, a draft and a cancelled order left out, refused without the permission); the purchasing journey checks the four tea still to come. |
| Purchase analysis | done | **Purchasing → Purchase analysis** (`/purchasing/analysis`, `g (`, permission `purchasing.order.read`, over `GET /api/v1/purchasing/reports/analysis?companyId&from&to&groupBy=month|supplier|item`): what was bought over a period, from approved, sent, received and closed orders (cancelled quantities and unapproved orders left out), valued at the net order price converted at each order's rate into the company's currency, with the received and invoiced parts of it, the number of orders and, by item, the quantity in the base unit. By month every month of the period is listed, empty ones as zero; by supplier and item the largest first. Totals on top; each row has a bar for its share of the largest row with the received part shaded (inline-size, so it mirrors in Arabic). Filters: company (with scope), period (default the last twelve months), supplier. EN/AR. Tests: a purchasing test (Alpha 1 000 in August and 500 + 180 in September with 500 received, Gamma 2 × 10 USD at 1 300 = 26 000, a cancelled and a draft order left out: by supplier Gamma then Alpha, by item 6 coffee for 26 180 and 15 tea for 1 500, by month 1 000 and 26 680, the supplier filter, invalid grouping and period refused); the purchasing journey checks Alpha's 18 000 ordered and 12 000 received. The demo's eleven months of history are orders closed without delivery, so they count as zero here; the live month carries the figures. |
| Supplier statement | done | **Payables → Open items → Statement** (over `GET /api/v1/payables/statement?companyId&partnerId&from&to`, permission `payables.open_item.read` with the member's company scope): a supplier's account for a period, per document currency: the balance brought forward (items posted before the period less their reversals before it), each invoice, debit note, advance and payment on account booked in the period and each reversal as its own line with the running balance, the increases and decreases, and the closing balance, which equals the open items' remaining amount for that supplier and currency at the period's end. Settlements and applications move money between the supplier's own items, so they do not change the balance and are not lines (the open items and the payment show them). The period defaults to the current month; document numbers open the document; the statement exports as an Excel workbook or CSV file (per currency: brought forward, the lines, the closing balance) through the audited export service, right to left in Arabic, to send to the supplier. EN/AR. Tests: a payables test (300 000 brought forward, an invoice and a debit note in the period, an invoice reversed: opening 300 000, increases 170 000, decreases 70 000, closing 400 000, the lines in date order with the reversal, the default period, an inverted period refused, refused without the permission); the payables journey reads Alpha's statement closing at the 3 000 the aging shows and downloads it as a workbook. |
| Idempotent writes from the web app | done | The web client (`src/api/client.ts`) puts a fresh `Idempotency-Key` (a version-4 UUID from `crypto.getRandomValues`, which also works on plain-HTTP deployments) on every POST, PUT, PATCH and DELETE, and when the network loses a write's answer (fetch rejects without a response) it sends the same request once more under the same key after 0.8 s, so the API answers with what it already did instead of doing it twice; reads, cancelled requests and HTTP errors are not resent, and a key the caller sets is kept. Two server fixes found on the way (ADR-0009 amended, A-135): an export's workbook sent under a key failed after it was delivered (the middleware tried to store the binary body as text, `invalid byte sequence 0x00`), and a `403 auth.step_up_required` was stored under the key, so the web app's replay after a step-up would have got the same refusal; now only JSON and empty answers are stored and 401/403 never are. Tests: two idempotency tests (an xlsx export under one key answered twice, both files whole; a write refused for a stale sign-in, stepped up, run under the same key once and replayed), five client tests (keys per write and none on reads, one resend with the same key and body, give-up after the second loss, no resend of reads, cancelled writes or answered ones, the caller's key kept). |
| Discussion follows company scopes | done | Comments, files, a record's timeline and its links now also follow the company of the record (A-136): each module that owns company-bound records answers which company they belong to through `IRecordCompanies` (Collaboration.Contracts; purchasing's eight document types, inventory's stock documents and warehouses, manual journals, bank payments and accounts, payment proposals, and a company itself), read from its own table with `RecordCompanyQuery` (Persistence), and the collaboration services check it against the scopes under which the member holds the record type's read permission: a list, a new comment or file, or a link on a record in a company outside those scopes answers `404 <type>.not_found` (whether it exists there is not revealed), a comment, file or link opened by id answers 404, and a record's links leave out those leading to another company's records; one lookup per record type, and none for owners and unscoped grants. Tests: a member reading only the North company sees North's discussion, not South's (list, add, edit, file list, upload and download, timeline, links, link removal and creation), North's link to South left out, then all of it once South is added to the role; every record type with a read permission is placed in a company except items and partners, and each module's lookup runs against its table. |
| Module boundary tests | done | ROADMAP 1.1 promised that a change crossing a module wall fails CI, and ARCHITECTURE §6 and ADR-0001 named tests for it, but no such test existed. `tests/Architecture/Quicker.Architecture.Tests` (in the solution, so in every CI run) now checks the project files and the compiled assemblies the API host loads: a module references only the kernel, the building blocks, its own projects and other modules' contracts; contracts reference only the kernel and nothing of persistence; the kernel and the building blocks know no module except `Identity.Contracts` (the caller); production code never references test support; dependencies point from process to core to platform modules and no new pair of modules comes to depend on each other, with the four deliberate exceptions named and explained (Inventory → Workflow, Numbering → Organization, Audit ↔ Identity, Inventory ↔ Items; ADR-0001 amended). The current code passes; a reference from Payables to the Purchasing implementation was added on trial and failed the test (`Quicker.Payables → Quicker.Purchasing (module)`). |
| Custom fields on purchasing documents | done | The API accepted custom fields on requisitions, orders, receipts, invoices, landed costs and returns, but the web app offered only companies: an administrator could not define a field for an order, nor anyone fill one in, and saving a document from its form sent no values, so it wiped any set through the API. Now `GET /api/v1/collaboration/custom-fields/hosts` lists the record types that carry custom fields, the Custom fields screen offers each of them (EN/AR names), and a shared `CustomFieldsFieldset` (every type: text with its maximum length, number, date, yes/no, choice, multiple choice as checkboxes, reference as an id) renders the active fields on the company form and on the six purchasing document forms, with the API's refusal of a value shown under its field; each document's detail shows its values read-only (`CustomFieldValuesList`: option labels, dates and numbers in the reader's format, lists joined for the language). Fixed on the way: receipts, invoices, landed costs and returns stored the custom-field values as sent rather than as the validator returned them (nulls kept, a multiple choice's repeated options kept, ids not in canonical form), unlike orders and requisitions. Tests: a purchasing test (a receipt, a return and an invoice with a multiple choice sent twice, a null and an upper-case id keep each option once, no null, the canonical id), the hosts endpoint in the custom-field test, and a Playwright journey (two fields added to purchase orders from the screen, the order refused without the required one with the message under the field, filled, shown on the order and kept through another edit, then read in Arabic). Reference fields take a record id; a picker per referenced type waits for a generic record search by type. |
| Custom fields on the other forms | done | The same fieldset and read-only values on the item form, the supplier form (on creation; the partner service keeps values when a later save sends none), manual journals, bank payments, stock adjustments and stock transfers; manual journals are now a custom-field host (the journal service already validated the values), and every host has its EN/AR name on the Custom fields screen. Forms of adjustments, transfers, journals and payments used to send no values on save, which cleared any set through the API. Bank payments stored the values as sent instead of as validated (the purchasing fix, here too). Tests: a banking test (a required field refused when missing, an unknown one refused, a multiple choice kept once per option), the hosts assertion covers journals and payments, and the custom-fields journey adds a field to items from the screen, fills it on an item and reads it back. |
| Work on the home page | done | The home page opens with **Your work today** for the chosen company (the company choice is shared with the other screens): approvals waiting for the person, deliveries past their date (with their open value per currency), supplier invoices on hold, what is overdue to suppliers today (with the total owed), cash and bank in the company's currency (the active accounts' balances) the items the planner says to reorder and the value of stock idle 90 days or more (its line count for people who may not read costs); counts that need action are shown in red, each card opens its screen, and a card appears only for people who may read what it counts. It reads the existing services (inbox, open order lines, invoices, aging at today, bank accounts, replenishment suggestions) and shares their cached answers with the screens. The workspace counts follow under their own heading. EN/AR (Arabic plurals). The payables journey reads the home page after its payments: 3 000 owed, the bank card, no approvals waiting. |
| Custom field default values | done | A custom field can carry a default value (migration `V0038`, `defaultValue` on the definition, checked against the field's type and options when saved, `custom_field.default_invalid` otherwise): whenever a record is saved without a value for the field, the default fills it (A-137), so a required field no longer refuses the drafts the system creates for someone, such as orders from replenishment suggestions. The published JSON Schema carries it as `default` and leaves a defaulted field out of `required`. The Custom fields screen sets it with the field's own control (a choice picks one of the options), and forms say under the field what it becomes if left empty. Tests: a collaboration test (a default outside the options refused, a company saved without the required field gets the default, a given value wins, the schema), the replenishment ordering test with a required order field defaulted to `normal`, and the custom-fields journey (a shelf field with a default, the hint on the item form). |
| Bilingual choices and help text on custom fields | done | The Custom fields screen saved a choice's options with English labels equal to their values and sent no help text, so editing a field there replaced Arabic option labels and descriptions set any other way. Each option now has its English and Arabic label beside it (English defaults to the value), the field has English and Arabic help text, and both are loaded when the field is opened, so an edit keeps them; forms and records show the option labels in the reader's language. The custom-fields journey gives the priority options Arabic labels, reopens the field to see them kept, and reads "عاجل" on the order in Arabic. |
| Attachment sweep | done | An attachment's file is stored before its record commits, so a save that failed afterwards left the file in storage forever. `IObjectStorage.ListAsync(prefix)` lists the objects under a prefix with when they were stored (the filesystem provider keeps the time in its sidecar, from the application clock; S3 reports it), and the platform job `collaboration.attachments.sweep` (schedule `collaboration.attachment_sweep`, daily at 02:40 UTC, seed `S0010`; for one workspace when queued inside it) removes each workspace's attachment files older than a day that no attachment record names, leaving retained objects and anything newer, which may belong to a save still in flight. Tests: listing on the filesystem provider (key order, prefix bounds, store time) and on S3 against MinIO in CI; the sweep keeps an attached file and a fresh orphan, removes an orphan a day old, and leaves another workspace's orphan to that workspace's sweep; a messaging test checks that every schedule the seeds ship names a job type the host runs, with a cron and a time zone that parse (a mistyped job type was tried and caught). |
| Custom field order and retirement | done | The Custom fields screen saved every field at position 0 and in use, so fields could not be ordered on forms nor taken out of use without deleting them; it now sets the order on the form and whether the field is in use. Taking a field out of use exposed a validator bug: it knew only fields in use, so saving any record that held a value for a retired field was refused as an unknown field. A retired field now keeps the values records hold (still checked against the field, no longer required or defaulted) and simply leaves the forms. Test: a company keeps its value for a retired field through a save, a new company gets no default from it, and the field comes back into use. |
| Slow-moving stock | done | **Inventory → Slow-moving stock** (`/inventory/slow-moving`, `g )`, permission `inventory.stock.read`, over `GET /api/v1/inventory/stock/slow-moving?companyId&asOf&idleDays&warehouseId`): own stock on hand at a date (not consignment) that has not been sold or consumed by an assembly for at least 30, 60, 90 (default), 180 or 365 days, counted from the last sale or consumption or, for stock never used, from when it first came in; transfers, adjustments, counts and returns move stock without making it used. Each line shows the item, warehouse, on hand in the base unit, first and last receipt, last sale or use, the idle days (red from 180) and the value at the date from the value entries on the inventory account, which, like the total, is shown only to people who may read costs; the member's company scope is enforced and lines in warehouses outside their scope are left out; a line opens its item and the grid exports. EN/AR. Tests: an inventory test (tea sold 10 days ago, jam never used with part moved to Basra, rice sold 2 days ago, salt sold out: idle 10 or more lists jam in Main for 18 days worth 150 then tea for 10 worth 180, total 330; without a minimum every line with stock in idle order; an earlier date as things stood then; a storekeeper without cost rights sees no values; an invalid threshold refused); the inventory journey sees nothing idle today and the never-sold water in both warehouses, worth 24 500, from a date 540 days ahead. |
| Trial balance: multi-dimension filter | done | **Accounting → Trial balance** (`/accounting/trial-balance`): the filter row is now a repeatable list instead of one fixed dimension/value pair — "Add filter" appends a row with its own dimension and value pickers, a row can be removed, and a dimension already used by another row is no longer offered, so the same dimension can't be filtered twice; the query sent to `GET /api/v1/accounting/reports/trial-balance` carries one `d.CODE=valueId` pair per row, ANDed, which the report endpoint and `InquiryService` already supported for any number of dimensions — only the screen had been limited to one. EN/AR. Tests: a web test adds a cost-centre filter then a project filter, confirms both are ANDed into the request, confirms the second row's dimension picker no longer offers the dimension the first row took, confirms the add button disables once every dimension is used, and confirms removing a row frees its dimension and drops it from the request. |
| Backup, restore and verify (10.3, first part) | done | Three migrator commands (A-138, runbook `docs/operations/backup-and-restore.md`, `make backup`/`restore`/`verify`). **`backup --out FILE`**: `pg_dump` of the whole database in custom format inside a snapshot the command exports, with `FILE.manifest.json` holding every table's row count in that same snapshot, the SHA-256, the owning role, the server version and the last migration; refused for a role subject to row security and never over an existing file. **`restore --from FILE --database NAME`**: refuses a file that no longer matches its hash, an existing database, another owning role or an older server; creates the roles if missing, restores in one transaction, compares every table with the manifest and runs the invariant harness over every active tenant; exits 0 only when all of it holds, and leaves a failed restore in place. **`verify [--tenant SLUG]`**: the harness as the daily job runs it, reading the deployment's anchor store. The PostgreSQL client tools are found for the server's version (`--pg-bin`, the Debian layout, the PATH); CI installs the PostgreSQL 17 client. First drill on the development database: 20.6 MB, 177 tables and 301,676 rows backed up in 7.6 s, restored and checked in 34 s, every table equal to its manifest, the demo tenant green on all 11 checks; it also surfaced open issue I4. Tests: the demo tenant backed up (manifest counts equal the source's), restored into a new database with no table differing, one tenant verified with every check passing, then a posted line removed from the restored books fails `entries_balanced`; a restore over an existing database, a file with one byte changed and an unsafe database name are refused without creating anything, and a second backup to the same file is refused. |
| Supplier invoice lines carry their dimensions to the journal | done | An invoice line's cost centre, department and project were stored but never reached the ledger, so cost-centre reporting missed every purchase expense. Now: a line takes `dimensions` (code → value, validated and turned into the shared set) or an existing `dimensionSetId` (checked, where it used to be taken on trust), never both; a line against an order line inherits the order line's set when it names none; goods and landed-cost lines refuse dimensions (they settle GRNI and landed-cost clearing, balance-sheet accounts that take the receipt's own); and the expense, service and return price-variance lines of the journal carry them, the payable line not. The invoice returns each line's `dimensions`. **Supplier invoices** in the web app offer a select per dimension under every expense, service and return line (an order line's empty choice reads "as ordered"), and the invoice shows them under the line. EN/AR. Tests: an API test (rent for head office on the fit-out project and a workshop cleaning service that inherits its order line's cost centre, both on the journal's expense lines at 1,300 IQD a dollar, the payable without; dimensions refused on a goods line, an unknown set, a set with values, and a value of another dimension; the harness green); the payables browser journey charges its rent to a cost centre and reads it back on the invoice, with axe on the form. |
| Costing methods locked once stock has moved (I2) | done | A company's costing method and cost pool, a category's costing override, an item's per-company override, and moving an item to a category with another method could all be changed through the API after stock had moved, re-valuing history without a document. Each is now refused once the stock it governs has moved (`company.costing_locked`, `category.costing_locked`, `item_settings.costing_locked`, `item.costing_locked`, each saying why), as Business Central refuses a costing-method change on an item with ledger entries (A-139); before any movement they stay free, and the rest of each record still saves. Inventory answers through `IStockActivity`, and Organization asks through `ICompanyCostingGuard`, which Inventory implements, so Organization takes no dependency on Inventory. Tests: an inventory test changes all four freely before stock moves, then after a receipt sees each refused, the company's other fields, the item's other settings and a move to a category with the same method still accepted, and the item's override in a company where it has not moved still free. |
| Journals keep the payables subledger (I4) | done | A manual journal could credit or debit the payables control account naming a supplier and create no open item, so the supplier's balance, aging and proposals missed it and the payables invariant failed (six journey workspaces showed it). Now, as with Business Central's vendor journal lines (A-140), the posting engine hands every line of a journal on a control account to the module that keeps that subledger (`IJournalSubledger`, implemented by Payables): the line must name a supplier (`journal.supplier_required`, checked before anything is written); posting opens the supplier's open item for it (owed to them for a credit, by them for a debit; an advance on the advances account, an opening item in an opening journal), keyed to the journal entry and due when the line says; reversing the entry, correcting the journal or its automatic reversal reverses the items, refused while one is settled (`payables.item_settled`). Entries of document modules (purchasing, banking, payables, inventory register themselves) can no longer be reversed or corrected through the journal-entry API (`journal_entry.owned_by_document`), which would have left the document's own subledger behind, and nobody posts in their name through it (`posting.source_module_reserved`). The demo books now let their payables journals open their items instead of opening them by hand; the purchase corrected in a closed month stays unpaid. Tests: a payables test (a customer and an unknown id refused; rent owed opens an item due on the line's date; a supplier credit by journal opens a negative item applied to the rent; the settled rent refuses reversal; an unsettled fee follows its reversal; an invoice's entry refused on the journal-entry API and a posting in purchasing's name refused; the harness green throughout); the engine's reversal test names a real supplier. |
| A receipt invoiced in parts at different prices | done | A receipt line invoiced by two invoices at different prices took the later price for every unit received: stock was valued at the second price, and goods received not invoiced kept a residue on a receipt fully invoiced, while the receipt line's own figures drifted with it so the harness saw nothing. Each invoice now settles its part only (A-141): the receipt's entries are worth what the invoices so far bill at their prices plus the part not yet invoiced at its expected cost, and a reversal takes back its own part, so a receipt invoiced in full is stocked at what the supplier billed. The costing engine's invoice settlement replaces only the direct cost, so landed costs and revaluations on the entry stay. Tests: a purchasing test (10 received at 2.00, 4 invoiced at 2.50 and 6 at 3.00: stock 28,600 then 36,400 with nothing left in GRNI; reversing the second brings back 28,600 and 15,600 waiting; the harness green at each step), which fails against the old code. |
| Same-day transfers under company-wide average cost (I3) | done | Receiving a two-step transfer on its ship date under company-wide average cost could abandon the receipt with `costing.reapplication_diverged`: within one cost pool the walk valued the transfer's inbound half before its outbound half, so the outbound cost included itself and every pass moved it again. A transfer inside one average pool now moves value without changing the average (A-141, as Business Central's average cost leaves transfers out): its outbound half is valued at the average without it and its inbound half takes that value right after, settling in one pass. Tests: an inventory test receives 35 of 35 at unit costs 30 and 1,500 and 20 of 35 with 5 short at 1,500, all on the ship date: each settles, every unit keeps its cost wherever it sits, nothing is left pending, the harness green. |
| A company-wide write-down with same-day movements (I1) | done | A net-realisable-value write-down for the whole company, dated a day that also had movements, was measured on the stock at the end of the day but applied at its start, so the day's issues left at the written-down cost and the stock ended off target (19,698 instead of 19,600 in the reproduction); and it was booked in full against the first warehouse. A revaluation now applies at the end of its date (a standard-cost change still applies at the start of its date, which is what it measures), and it is spread over the warehouses holding the stock by quantity, each share on its own inventory or in-transit account; the IAS 2 ceiling reads each revaluation once however many shares it has (A-141). Tests: an inventory test (100 in at 250, 30 moved and 2 counted missing the same day, written down to 200: 98 units, −4,900, 19,600 on hand and in the ledger, main 68 units −3,400 and the branch 30 units −1,500, the next day's issue at 200, the harness green). |
| The harness after the browser journeys | done | The invariant harness ran after every backend scenario but never over what the browser journeys leave behind, which is how the journal-lines journey broke the payables invariant (I4) unnoticed while CI stayed green. The CI browser job now ends with `verify` over every workspace the journeys created, reading the anchors the API wrote (both pinned to one absolute anchor folder), and fails the build on any failing check. Rehearsed locally the way CI runs it: a fresh database with the demo seeded, all 29 journeys, then `verify`: 38 workspaces, every check passing. |
